170.(1) Aggarwal v. DEA, No. 22-1718 (9th Cir. 2023) (requiring DEA to reconsider denial of psilocybin for end-of-life therapeutic use under Right-to-Try Act). (2) DEA transmittal of psilocybin rescheduling petition to HHS for scientific and medical evaluation (August 11, 2025). Following the 9th Circuit ruling, DEA formally transmitted the petition by Dr. Sunil Aggarwal and the Advanced Integrative Medical Science Institute (AIMS) — requesting psilocybin rescheduling from Schedule I to Schedule II — to the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services for the eight-factor scientific and medical evaluation required under 21 U.S.C. § 811. HHS has not publicly disclosed a response or timeline as of May 2026. A Schedule II rescheduling would, per practitioner commentary, materially change the calculation under various Right-to-Try laws and expand research access. The transmittal is separate from and parallel to the product-specific rescheduling that would occur upon FDA approval of COMP360 or another psilocybin product. DEA is not required to act on HHS's recommendation within any specified timeline. Open source ↗
171.Definium Therapeutics, Inc. (Nasdaq: DFTX), 2026 Pipeline and Commercial Readiness Update (January 2026). DT402 is Definium's early-stage program in autism spectrum disorder (ASD): an oral lysergide formulation distinct from DT120 ODT, designed for longer-duration exposure in a population with specific 5-HT2A receptor sensitivity considerations. Phase 2a ASD data expected in 2026. Significance for practitioners: (a) if positive, DT402 would represent the first psychedelic-adjacent controlled trial signal in ASD, potentially intersecting with pediatric mental health considerations; (b) the indication profile (pediatric, neurodevelopmental) raises distinct safety considerations from DT120's anxiety/depression indications; (c) Definium's pipeline thus spans GAD (DT120, Phase 3), MDD (DT120, Phase 3), PTSD (DT120, Haven, planned 2027), and ASD (DT402, Phase 2a 2026). Open source ↗
172.David E. Nichols, Hallucinogens, 101 Pharmacology & Therapeutics 131 (2004). PMID 14761703; DOI 10.1016/j.pharmthera.2003.11.002. Definitive review: classic psychedelics are partial 5-HT2A/2C/1A agonists; “considered physiologically safe and do not produce dependence or addiction”; confirms onset/duration for psilocybin (20–40 min onset, 4–6 hr), LSD (30–90 min onset, 8–12 hr), DMT (seconds inhaled), mescaline (45–90 min onset, 8–12 hr); no neurotoxicity at therapeutic doses; mechanism via 5-HT2A neocortical pyramidal cells.
173.Carlos A. Zarate, Jr. et al., A Randomized Trial of an N-Methyl-D-Aspartate Antagonist in Treatment-Resistant Major Depression, 63 Archives of General Psychiatry 856 (2006). DOI 10.1001/archpsyc.63.8.856. Randomized placebo-controlled crossover trial (n=18 TRD patients): single sub-anesthetic ketamine infusion (0.5 mg/kg IV, 40 min) produced rapid antidepressant effects within hours, sustained up to one week; effects emerged by 110 minutes post-infusion on HDRS. First TRD-specific ketamine trial confirming rapid onset; foundational for the esketamine (Spravato) approval.
174.Xaver Koenig and Karlheinz Hilber, The Anti-Addiction Drug Ibogaine and the Heart: A Delicate Relation, 20 Molecules 2208 (2015). PMID 25642835; DOI 10.3390/molecules20022208. Ibogaine blocks hERG (IKr) potassium channels, prolonging QT interval and creating risk for life-threatening Torsade de Pointes arrhythmia; 19 fatalities temporally associated with ibogaine documented between 1990–2008 (six from cardiac causes); subsequent additional fatalities and QTc prolongation cases accumulated. CYP2D6 poor metabolizer status elevates cardiac risk. Mandatory protocol: baseline 12-lead ECG, IV magnesium prophylaxis, continuous telemetry for full 12–36 hour acute period.
175.Caitlin Elizabeth Hughes and Alex Stevens, What Can We Learn From The Portuguese Decriminalization of Illicit Drugs?, 50 British Journal of Criminology 999 (2010). DOI 10.1093/bjc/azq038. Primary empirical analysis of Portugal’s July 1, 2001 drug decriminalization (Law 30/2000): possession of all drugs decriminalized up to a 10-day personal supply threshold; handled by Dissuasion Commissions (health/social workers) rather than criminal prosecution. Findings: “Portuguese decriminalization did not lead to major increases in drug use”; problematic use decreased; HIV transmission among people who inject drugs fell significantly; criminal justice overcrowding reduced; drug-related deaths declined. Drawing on independent evaluations and 13 stakeholder interviews in 2007 and 2009, comparing trends against Spain and Italy.
176.P.S. Chu et al., The Destruction of the Lower Urinary Tract by Ketamine Abuse: A New Syndrome?, 102 BJU International 1616 (2008). PMID 18680495; DOI 10.1111/j.1464-410X.2008.07920.x. Case series (n=59 regular ketamine users presenting with lower urinary tract symptoms without bacterial UTI, stone, or malignancy): all biopsies showed epithelial inflammation and neovascularization; clinical features include severe suprapubic pain, urinary frequency, urgency, urge incontinence, dysuria, and haematuria; in advanced cases: hydronephrosis, ureteral stricture, vesicoureteral reflux, and renal failure; potentially irreversible in severe cases requiring surgical intervention; cessation of ketamine use early is essential. First large-series description of ketamine-induced uropathy. See also Shahani et al. (2007, Urology 69:810), which first named ketamine-associated ulcerative cystitis as a new clinical entity.
177.Steven A. Barker, N,N-Dimethyltryptamine (DMT), an Endogenous Hallucinogen: Past, Present, and Future Research to Determine Its Role and Function, 12 Frontiers in Neuroscience 536 (2018). PMID 30127713; PMC6088236; DOI 10.3389/fnins.2018.00536. Comprehensive review confirming DMT is synthesized from tryptamine by indolethylamine-N-methyltransferase (INMT) in human brain, lung, blood, and cerebrospinal fluid; INMT activity detected in human brain subcortical layers (Saavedra et al. 1973); DMT measured in human bodily fluids since Bumpus and Page (1955). DMT’s physiological role remains under investigation; proposed functions include putative neurotransmitter. Note: endogenous biosynthesis is confirmed; relationship to naturally occurring altered states of consciousness is not yet established.
178.Jordi Riba et al., Human Pharmacology of Ayahuasca: Subjective and Cardiovascular Effects, Monoamine Metabolite Excretion, and Pharmacokinetics, 306 Journal of Pharmacology and Experimental Therapeutics 73 (2003). PMID 12660312; DOI 10.1124/jpet.103.049882. Definitive human pharmacokinetics study (n=18, double-blind placebo-controlled): ayahuasca combines DMT (5-HT2A agonist) with harmine, harmaline, and tetrahydroharmine (beta-carbolines acting as reversible MAO-A inhibitors, predominantly peripherally at gastrointestinal/hepatic level); MAO inhibition prevents first-pass DMT metabolism and enables oral bioavailability; DMT Cmax 12–17 ng/mL; Tmax median 1.5 h; peak subjective effects 1.5–2 h; duration 4–6 h; significant diastolic BP increase (+9 mm Hg at high dose). Drug-drug interaction: MAO inhibition contraindicates SSRIs, SNRIs, tramadol, decongestants, tyramine-rich foods (serotonin syndrome or hypertensive crisis risk).
179.Deborah C. Mash et al., Ibogaine Detoxification Transitions Opioid and Cocaine Abusers Between Dependence and Abstinence: Clinical Observations and Treatment Outcomes, 9 Frontiers in Pharmacology 529 (2018). PMID 29922156; PMC5996271; DOI 10.3389/fphar.2018.00529. Open-label case series (n=191; medical monitoring at licensed clinic; whole blood pharmacokinetics measured); no significant adverse events at effective dose range for blocking opioid withdrawal; ibogaine significantly diminished heroin and cocaine cravings on multi-dimensional questionnaires; improvements in health and mood at program discharge. This is the largest peer-reviewed clinical case series from the Mash Bahamas cohort. Critical limitations: no control arm, open-label design, self-selected population, high self-selection bias, variable follow-up; results support product development interest but do not establish efficacy; controlled RCTs required.
180.Peter J. Neumann, Joshua T. Cohen, and Milton C. Weinstein, Updating Cost-Effectiveness—The Curious Resilience of the $50,000-per-QALY Threshold, 371 New England Journal of Medicine 796 (2014). PMID 25162885; DOI 10.1056/NEJMp1405158. Authoritative analysis: the $50,000/QALY benchmark originated from 1970s dialysis cost estimates and persisted without theoretical justification; evidence suggests it “is too low and might best be thought of as an implied lower boundary.” Current U.S. practice: ICER (Institute for Clinical and Economic Review) uses $100,000–$150,000/QALY as its value-based pricing threshold; this is the range referred to as “commonly used U.S. cost-effectiveness threshold.” WHO-CHOICE: 1–3× GDP per capita (~$50,000–$150,000 for the U.S.). Against that benchmark, the psilocybin cost-effectiveness evidence sits high rather than low: Avanceña et al. (2025) (Note 57) report $117,517/QALY over a 12-month horizon, near the top of the $50,000–$150,000 range and cost-effective with 75% probability only at the $150,000 threshold.
181.American Civil Liberties Union, A Tale of Two Countries: Racially Targeted Arrests in the Era of Marijuana Reform (April 20, 2020), available at https://www.aclu.org/report/tale-two-countries-racially-targeted-arrests-era-marijuana-reform (2010–2018 national arrest data, FBI Uniform Crime Reports). Key findings: Black people are 3.64 times more likely than white people to be arrested for marijuana possession despite comparable usage rates; disparity exists in every state; in 31 states, disparities were larger in 2018 than 2010; legalization reduced total arrests but not racial disparities in most states. Documents the mechanism of War on Drugs racial targeting: racially biased policing (stop-and-frisk, search, and arrest based on race rather than reasonable suspicion) disproportionately funnels Black people into the criminal legal system for drug offenses, creating the historical trauma and persistent disparities in criminal justice consequences documented in Section XIX. See also ACLU (2013), The War on Marijuana in Black and White (2001–2010 data; Black people 3.73× more likely to be arrested).
182.Marissa B. Esser et al., Deaths from Excessive Alcohol Use—United States, 2016–2021, 73 MMWR Morbidity and Mortality Weekly Report 154 (February 29, 2024). DOI 10.15585/mmwr.mm7308a1. CDC analysis of 58 alcohol-attributable causes of death: average of approximately 140,000 alcohol-attributable deaths per year in the U.S. (2016–2021); deaths rose approximately 29% from 2016–17 to 2020–21 due largely to COVID-19 factors; alcohol-related causes include liver disease, acute injury, and other conditions. Confirmed also by CDC Fact Sheet: “Excessive alcohol use is responsible for more than 140,000 deaths in the United States each year.” Available at https://www.cdc.gov/alcohol/fact-sheets/states/excessive-alcohol-use-united-states.html.
183.Oregon Health Authority, Oregon Psilocybin Services Quarterly Data (Q1 2025, published June 2025); Psychedelic Alpha, The Oregon Psilocybin Services Tracker (updated Q3 2025, citing OHA data), https://psychedelicalpha.com/data/the-oregon-psilocybin-services-tracker; Oregon Public Broadcasting (December 14, 2024): “an individual session might run $1,000 or more, although group sessions might cost less,” https://www.opb.org/article/2024/12/14/psilocybin-industry-fine-tuning-2025; Psychedelic Support (2025): $1,200–$3,000 for individuals, from $750 for groups; Althea (July 2025): $1,000–$3,000 or higher for individual sessions; group sessions $300–$500. Insurance does not cover psilocybin therapy as of May 2026. Cost reflects: facilitator fee, service center operational costs (including $10,000 annual OHA license fee), psilocybin product ($25 for up to 50 mg including taxes), and mandated security and storage infrastructure. Multiple providers offer sliding-scale pricing; nonprofit subsidies available (Sheri Eckert Foundation, Forward Fund).
184.Food and Drug Omnibus Reform Act of 2022 (FDORA), Pub. L. No. 117-328, sections 3601–3602 (December 29, 2022), codified at 21 U.S.C. sections 355(z) and 360j(g). Requires sponsors of Phase 3 clinical trials to submit Diversity Action Plans (DAPs) for enrollment of underrepresented racial, ethnic, sex, and age group populations. FDA issued implementing guidance: (1) Draft Guidance, Dkt. FDA-2022-D-1984 (April 13, 2022)—“Diversity Plans to Improve Enrollment of Participants From Underrepresented Racial and Ethnic Populations in Clinical Trials”; (2) Updated Draft Guidance, Dkt. FDA-2024-D-2716 (June 28, 2024)—“Diversity Action Plans to Improve Enrollment of Participants from Underrepresented Populations in Clinical Studies.” Note: the June 2024 guidance was removed from the FDA website in early 2026 following Executive Order on DEI; the underlying statutory requirement at 21 U.S.C. section 355(z) remains in force as an Act of Congress. Sponsors with Phase 3 psychedelic INDs should confirm current FDA expectations directly with their assigned project manager given the guidance withdrawal.
185.United Nations Convention on Psychotropic Substances (1971), opened for signature February 21, 1971, entered into force August 16, 1976, 1019 U.N.T.S. 175, available at https://www.unodc.org/unodc/en/treaties/psychotropics.html. Schedules psilocybin (Schedule I), LSD (Schedule I), DMT (Schedule I), MDMA (Schedule I), and mescaline (Schedule I). Article 32(4) provides an exemption for traditional indigenous use of certain plants containing scheduled substances. Treaty generally prohibits member states from going below the scheduling floor, but permits research and authorized medical access within a prescription system. Key nuances: (1) Australian Schedule 8 rescheduling (July 2023) was accomplished as authorized prescriber medical access within a therapeutic goods framework, permissible under the treaty; (2) FDA approval of psilocybin would establish “accepted medical use,” enabling Schedule III rescheduling under 21 U.S.C. section 811(d)(1) consistent with treaty obligations; (3) Bolivia and Peru have reserved traditional-use recognition for ayahuasca and peyote.
186.Patrick C. Dolder, Yasmin Schmid, Manuel Haschke, Katharina M. Rentsch, and Matthias E. Liechti, Pharmacokinetics and Concentration-Effect Relationship of Oral LSD in Humans, 19 International Journal of Neuropsychopharmacology 1 (2016, article pyv072). PMID 26108222; PMC4772267; DOI 10.1093/ijnp/pyv072. First published pharmacokinetic data for oral LSD in humans (n=16, 8M/8F, double-blind, 200 μg oral dose). Plasma LSD quantifiable up to 12 hours post-dose; Cmax 4.5±1.4 ng/mL reached at median 1.5 h (range 0.5–4 h); half-life 3.6±0.9 h up to 12 h, terminal t½ 8.9±5.9 h. Acute subjective and sympathomimetic responses lasted up to 12 hours, closely correlated with plasma concentrations, with no acute tolerance. No sex differences in PK profiles. 1% excreted unchanged in urine; 13% as 2-oxo-3-hydroxy-LSD within 24 h. These data confirm the 8–12 h duration of oral LSD and the 30–90 min onset (first-pass effects beginning within 30 min, Cmax at ~1.5 h) documented in the LSD/DT120 section of this toolkit.
187.Una D. McCann et al. (George A. Ricaurte, corresponding author), Quantitative PET Studies of the Serotonin Transporter in MDMA Users and Controls Using [11C]McN5652 and [11C]DASB, 30 Neuropsychopharmacology 1741 (2005). PMID 15841106; PMC2034411; DOI 10.1038/sj.npp.1300736. Quantitative PET study (n=23 abstinent recreational MDMA users vs. 19 non-MDMA controls) using validated first- and second-generation SERT ligands: global SERT reductions found in MDMA users across all SERT-binding parameters and both PET ligands; reductions in selected cortical and subcortical structures on preplanned regional analyses. Exploratory analyses suggested SERT measures recover with time and that SERT loss correlates with MDMA use intensity. ‘These quantitative PET data…provide strong evidence of reduced SERT density in some recreational MDMA users.’ Critical note: these findings document SERT reduction associated with recreational use; the causal mechanism and dose-threshold for neurotoxicity in humans remain debated. MDMA is categorically distinct from classic psychedelics on this dimension.
188.Gerald Thomas et al. (including Philippe Lucas and Kenneth W. Tupper), Ayahuasca-Assisted Therapy for Addiction: Results from a Preliminary Observational Study in Canada, 6 Current Drug Abuse Reviews 30 (2013). PMID 23627784; DOI 10.2174/15733998113099990003. Preliminary observational study (n=12 participants; rural First Nations community, British Columbia, Canada; ‘Working with Addiction and Stress’ retreats combining four days group counselling with two expert-led ayahuasca ceremonies; pre-treatment and six-month follow-up data). Statistically significant improvements (p<0.05) in hopefulness, empowerment, mindfulness, and quality-of-life meaning/outlook subscales. Self-reported alcohol, tobacco, and cocaine use declined; cannabis and opiate use did not; cocaine reduction statistically significant. All 12 participants reported positive and lasting changes at six-month follow-up. Critical limitations: small n=12; no control arm; observational design; self-selected sample in a ceremonial/counselling context; results must not be generalized beyond this specific population and setting. This is the primary evidence for ayahuasca-assisted therapy benefiting Indigenous substance use populations.
189.James Jonathan Rucker et al. (King’s College London / Beckley Psytech), Phase 1, Placebo-Controlled, Single Ascending Dose Trial to Evaluate the Safety, Pharmacokinetics and Effect on Altered States of Consciousness of Intranasal BPL-003 (5-Methoxy-N,N-Dimethyltryptamine Benzoate) in Healthy Participants, 38 Journal of Psychopharmacology 712 (2024). PMID 38616411; PMC11311898; DOI 10.1177/02698811241246857. Phase 1 single ascending dose (n=44 psychedelic-naïve; 1–12 mg BPL-003 intranasal, double-blind, placebo-controlled; NCT05347849). Key pharmacokinetic findings: 5-MeO-DMT rapidly absorbed and eliminated; median time to peak plasma concentration approximately 8–10 min; mean terminal elimination half-life <27 min; dose-proportional systemic exposure; plasma bufotenine negligible. Pharmacodynamic: 60% of participants had ‘complete mystical experience’ at 10 and 12 mg; rapid onset and short-lasting duration. No serious adverse events; most common AEs: nasal discomfort, nausea, headache, vomiting. Provides the human pharmacokinetic reference for intranasal 5-MeO-DMT (BPL-003 formulation); the inhaled/vaporized form (toad secretion or synthetic) has even faster onset given buccal/pulmonary absorption.
190.Emily R. Stern et al. (Mount Sinai School of Medicine), Resting-State Functional Connectivity Between Fronto-Parietal and Default Mode Networks in Obsessive-Compulsive Disorder, 7 PLoS One e36356 (2012). PMID 22570705; PMC3343054; DOI 10.1371/journal.pone.0036356. fMRI study (n=30 OCD patients [17 unmedicated] and 32 control subjects [17 unmedicated]). OCD associated with altered connectivity between fronto-parietal network (FPN) seeds and multiple DMN regions (posterior cingulate cortex, medial frontal cortex, posterior inferior parietal lobule, parahippocampus). Key finding quoted in this toolkit’s Section II: ‘The default mode network has been found to be hyperactive in OCD during cognitive tasks’ (abstract). Patients exhibited reduced negative correlations between FPN and DMN — reflecting an inability to disengage from internally-generated scenarios when executing external tasks. OCD phenotype includes persistent intrusive internally-generated thoughts that the DMN normally generates; impaired FPN–DMN antagonism may mechanistically explain core OCD symptoms.
191.Albert Most (pseudonym of Ken Nelson), Bufo Alvarius: The Psychedelic Toad of the Sonoran Desert (Venom Press, Denton, Texas, 1984). OCLC: 79477238. Self-published pamphlet (approximately 8 pages) describing how to collect and vaporize the venom of the Sonoran Desert toad (Incilius alvarius, formerly Bufo alvarius) to produce psychoactive effects from its 5-MeO-DMT content. This pamphlet is the documented origin of the modern practice of toad vaporization: it ‘was responsible for the start of the recreational use of the toad venom and of 5-MeO-DMT’ (Wikipedia, citing Villa 2023, Weil & Davis 1994, Siebert 2021, and Ermakova, Dunbar & Rucker 2022). See also Andrew Weil and Wade Davis, The Importance of Being Bufo, Natural History Magazine (1994), documenting the spread of the practice. No evidence of pre-1984 ceremonial use of Incilius alvarius venom by any indigenous group has been documented in anthropological or archaeological literature. The current ceremonies performed in Mexico and elsewhere represent a late-20th-century syncretic tradition, not an ancient indigenous one. Toad vaporization practice creates significant conservation pressure on wild Incilius alvarius populations. Synthetic 5-MeO-DMT avoids this harm entirely.
192.U.S. Department of Justice, United States Attorney’s Office, Central District of California. (1) Five Defendants, Including Two Doctors, Charged in Connection with Actor Matthew Perry’s Fatal Drug Overdose Last Year (August 15, 2024), available at https://www.justice.gov/usao-cdca/pr/five-defendants-including-two-doctors-charged-connection-actor-matthew-perrys-fatal; (2) North Hollywood Drug Dealer Who Sold Ketamine that Killed Actor Matthew Perry Sentenced to 15 Years in Federal Prison (April 8, 2026), available at https://www.justice.gov/usao-cdca/pr/north-hollywood-drug-dealer-who-sold-ketamine-killed-actor-matthew-perry-sentenced-15; (3) Matthew Perry’s Former Live-In Personal Assistant Sentenced to Nearly 3½ Years (May 2026). LA County Medical Examiner: Perry died October 28, 2023, from ‘acute effects of ketamine’ (plus drowning, coronary artery disease, and buprenorphine effects). All five defendants convicted and sentenced: Jasveen Sangha (‘Ketamine Queen’): 15 years (April 8, 2026); Dr. Salvador Plasencia: 30 months (December 2025); Kenneth Iwamasa (personal assistant who administered fatal injections): 41 months (May 2026); Eric Fleming: 24 months (April 2026); Dr. Mark Chavez: 3 years probation + 8 months home detention (October 2024). Core legal failure: ketamine supplied and self-administered without physician present, without monitoring, and without adequate informed consent — confirming criminal exposure for negligent unsupervised ketamine administration. Sangha charged $50,000 for ~50 vials; Iwamasa injected Perry with at least three shots on October 28, 2023 without medical training.
193.International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN) Red List: Lophophora williamsii (peyote cactus). IUCN status: Vulnerable (assessed 2013, last updated). Threats: habitat loss (conversion to agriculture, urban development, energy infrastructure), over-harvesting for legal NAC religious trade, illegal poaching, feral pig damage. Texas: state-listed as endangered (over-harvested). Mexico: listed as ‘subject to special protection’ under NOM-059-SEMARNAT-2010. Available at https://www.iucnredlist.org/. Cactus Conservation Institute (CCI): only longitudinal study of harvesting impact on wild peyote (Terry et al. 2011, 2012, Journal of the Botanical Research Institute of Texas): survival rate of harvested plants (73%) significantly lower than unharvested controls (94%); average harvested mass per plant decreased by 44% between first and second harvests, then 32% further, confirming unsustainable harvest rates. Available at https://cactusconservation.org/cci-research/regrowth-long-term-impact-of-harvesting-on-peyote/. See also Ermakova et al. (2020, bioRxiv) ecology survey: ‘reports dating back 35 years already noted declining populations resulting in shortages of supply for the NAC;’ current rates of harvest are ‘management by extirpation.’ Non-Native practitioners should use San Pedro (Echinopsis pachanoi, widely available, unscheduled, not threatened), which contains comparable mescaline concentrations.
194.Yvette I. Sheline, Deanna M. Barch, Joseph L. Price, Marcus E. Raichle et al. (Washington University, St. Louis), The Default Mode Network and Self-Referential Processes in Depression, 106 Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences 1942 (2009). PMID 19171889; PMC2631078; DOI 10.1073/pnas.0812686106. fMRI study of major depression and DMN: depressed patients exhibited (1) failure to normally down-regulate activity broadly within the DMN [vmPFC/BA10, anterior cingulate/BA24/32, lateral parietal/BA39, lateral temporal/BA21] during both passive negative-picture viewing and active reappraisal; and (2) significantly greater stimulus-induced DMN activation (amygdala, parahippocampus, hippocampus) compared to controls. Conclusion: ‘depression is characterized by both stimulus-induced heightened activity and a failure to normally down-regulate activity broadly within the DMN.’ This provides the primary empirical basis for the toolkit’s claim that DMN hyperactivation is a documented feature of treatment-resistant depression; it precedes and mechanistically grounds psilocybin’s demonstrated post-treatment reversal of this pattern (Carhart-Harris 2017, Note 99).
195.Samantha J. Broyd, Charmaine Demanuele, Stefan Debener, Suzannah K. Helps, Christopher J. James, and Edmund J. S. Sonuga-Barke, Default-Mode Brain Dysfunction in Mental Disorders: A Systematic Review, 33 Neuroscience & Biobehavioral Reviews 279 (2009). PMID 18824195; DOI 10.1016/j.neubiorev.2008.09.002. Systematic review of DMN dysfunction across psychiatric and neurodevelopmental disorders. Covers dementia, schizophrenia, epilepsy, anxiety, depression, autism, and ADHD. Core finding: impaired default-mode network activity — specifically, failure to suppress DMN during externally-directed cognitive tasks, altered DMN connectivity, and altered psychological functions served by the DMN — is a transdiagnostic feature of multiple mental disorders. The review establishes that abnormal DMN regulation is not disorder-specific but rather a common pathway through which diverse psychiatric conditions express impairment in internally-directed cognition, self-referential processing, and mind-wandering. This provides the broader literature base for the toolkit’s claim that DMN hyperactivation characterizes the conditions for which psychedelic therapy is being investigated (depression, PTSD, OCD, and addiction).
196.Lorenz Mueller et al. (Matthias E. Liechti, corresponding author; University Hospital Basel), Pharmacokinetics, Pharmacodynamics, and Urinary Recovery of Oral Mescaline Hydrochloride in Healthy Participants, 64 Clinical Pharmacokinetics 1495 (2025). PMID 40658345; PMC12479620; DOI 10.1007/s40262-025-01544-x. First detailed pharmacokinetic-pharmacodynamic characterization of mescaline in humans (n=49 participants; 105 single-dose administrations of 100–800 mg oral mescaline HCl; two Phase 1 trials). Key PK findings: peak plasma concentration at geometric mean 2.0 h; half-life 3.5 h; dose-proportional increases in total exposure and Cmax; 53% excreted unchanged in urine (oral bioavailability at least 53%). PD findings: mean model-predicted onset of ‘any drug effect’ approximately 1 hour post-dose; maximum effect intensity and duration increase with dose, ranging from 13% intensity/2.8 h at 100 mg to 89% intensity/15 h at 800 mg. These data confirm the toolkit’s ‘onset 45–90 min, duration 8–12 hrs’ for therapeutic-range doses of mescaline. The study also confirms that mescaline’s longer duration relative to psilocybin reflects its slower t½ (3.5 h vs. psilocin’s ~1.5 h half-life) and is consistent with traditional ceremonial context (full-day ceremonies with huachuma).
197.Marta Di Forti et al. (EU-GEI Work Package 2 Group), The Contribution of Cannabis Use to Variation in the Incidence of Psychotic Disorder Across Europe (EU-GEI): A Multicentre Case-Control Study, 6 Lancet Psychiatry 427 (2019). PMID 30902669; PMC7646282; DOI 10.1016/S2215-0366(19)30048-3. The most rigorous epidemiological study of cannabis and psychosis: n=901 first-episode psychosis patients across 11 EU sites and Brazil + 1,237 population controls, May 2010–April 2015. Key findings: (1) Daily cannabis use: adjusted OR 3.2 (95% CI 2.2–4.1) for psychotic disorder vs. never-users; (2) Daily high-potency cannabis use: OR 4.8 (95% CI 2.5–6.3); (3) Population attributable fraction: if high-potency cannabis were no longer available, 12.2% (95% CI 3.0–16.1) of first-episode psychosis cases could be prevented across 11 sites (rising to 30% in London and 50% in Amsterdam); (4) Adjusted incidence rates of psychotic disorder correlated positively with prevalence of high-potency cannabis use across sites (r=0.7; p=0.029). Critical scientific nuance: epidemiological association is robust; causation vs. selection bias remains contested. The association is specifically concentrated in: predisposed individuals (genetic risk factors for psychosis); daily high-potency (THC≥10%) use; and adolescent-onset use. Moderate, infrequent, adult-onset cannabis use does not carry the same psychosis signal.
198.Johannes Reckweg et al. (Maastricht University / GH Research), A Phase 1, Dose-Ranging Study to Assess Safety and Psychoactive Effects of a Vaporized 5-Methoxy-N,N-Dimethyltryptamine Formulation (GH001) in Healthy Volunteers, 12 Frontiers in Pharmacology 760671 (2021). PMID 34912222; PMC8667866; DOI 10.3389/fphar.2021.760671. Phase 1 dose-ranging study (n=22 healthy volunteers; vaporized GH001 administered by inhalation at 2, 6, 12, and 18 mg single dose and individualized dose escalation). Key PK/PD findings: ultra-rapid onset (described as ‘seconds’ onset for vaporized/inhaled route); short duration of psychedelic effects (consistent with the document’s ‘20–40 min’ characterization); higher doses produced significantly greater psychedelic experience ratings on PES, MEQ, EDI, and 5D-ASC. Adverse events were mild and resolved spontaneously; no vital sign effects at 1 or 3 hours post-administration. This Phase 1 study established the safety and dose-response foundation for subsequent GH001 Phase 2 and Phase 2b efficacy trials. Distinguishing from BPL-003 (Note 189): GH001 (GH Research) is an inhaled/vaporized formulation with onset in seconds and duration ~11–40 min for the psychedelic experience. BPL-003 (AtaiBeckley) is an intranasal benzoate salt formulation with peak plasma concentration at ~8–10 min and terminal t½ <27 min. Both are mebufotenin (5-MeO-DMT) but different routes and formulations with different pharmacokinetic profiles.
199.Reserved. No source is recorded at this number, and no claim in the toolkit cites it. The numbering is left unchanged so that every other endnote keeps its published number.
200.Madeline H. Meier et al. (Terrie E. Moffitt, corresponding author; Duke University / University of Otago, New Zealand), Persistent Cannabis Users Show Neuropsychological Decline from Childhood to Midlife, 109 Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences E2657 (2012). PMID 22927402; PMC3479587; DOI 10.1073/pnas.1206820109. Published August 27, 2012 (online ahead of print); October 2, 2012 (final). Prospective longitudinal cohort study: Dunedin Study birth cohort (n=1,037 born 1972/1973 in Dunedin, New Zealand; followed from birth to age 38). Cannabis use assessed at ages 18, 21, 26, 32, and 38; neuropsychological testing at age 13 (before initiation) and age 38 (after establishment of use patterns). Key findings: persistent cannabis use beginning in adolescence was associated with broad neuropsychological decline across multiple domains; those who began as adults showed no significant decline; the 8-point IQ decline was concentrated in adolescent-onset persistent users (those meeting cannabis dependence criteria at 3+ assessments); cessation did not fully restore neuropsychological functioning in adolescent-onset users. Important controversies: Rogeberg (2013, PNAS) argued the association could reflect confounding from socioeconomic status; Meier et al. (2013 Reply) reanalyzed and maintained the causal interpretation. The IQ finding remains ‘the strongest longitudinal data available’ for this claim while acknowledging the debate. Primary relevance: supports adolescent-specific cannabis risk communication in destigmatization contexts; does NOT generalize to adult-onset use.
201.Catalina Lopez-Quintero et al. (Carlos Blanco, corresponding author; New York State Psychiatric Institute, Columbia University), Probability and Predictors of Transition from First Use to Dependence on Nicotine, Alcohol, Cannabis, and Cocaine: Results of the National Epidemiologic Survey on Alcohol and Related Conditions (NESARC), 115 Drug and Alcohol Dependence 120 (2011). PMID 21145178; PMC3069146; DOI 10.1016/j.drugalcdep.2010.11.004. Prospective analysis of 7,389 cannabis users in the NESARC nationally representative survey (Wave 1 and Wave 2). Key findings: cumulative probability of transition to cannabis dependence was 8.9% (the source for the document’s ‘9%’ lifetime cannabis user dependence figure); median time from first use to dependence: approximately 5 years. Cannabis had the lowest probability of dependence among the four substances studied (nicotine 67.5%, cocaine 20.9%, alcohol 22.7%, cannabis 8.9%). Significant racial-ethnic differences in transition probability were observed. Note on the ‘17% of those who begin in adolescence’ figure: this higher figure for adolescent-onset users appears in Winters & Lee (2008, Drug Alcohol Depend 86:187-193) and is cited in NASEM 2017 report (Note 202). The Lopez-Quintero paper does not specifically report the adolescent-onset subgroup rate, but the 17% figure is consistent with the broader literature showing elevated dependence risk for early-onset use.
202.National Academies of Sciences, Engineering, and Medicine (2017). The Health Effects of Cannabis and Cannabinoids: The Current State of Evidence and Recommendations for Research. Washington, DC: The National Academies Press. DOI 10.17226/24625. Available at https://www.nap.edu/catalog/24625. 487-page report commissioned by Congress following 20+ years of research suppression. Evidence grading system: conclusive, substantial, moderate, limited, no/insufficient evidence. Key findings most relevant to this toolkit: (1) Chronic pain: CONCLUSIVE evidence that cannabis/cannabinoids are effective for treatment of chronic pain in adults (the evidence base: multiple RCTs showing statistically significant reduction in pain scores, primarily neuropathic pain); (2) Chemotherapy-induced nausea and vomiting: CONCLUSIVE evidence for oral cannabinoids; (3) MS spasticity: SUBSTANTIAL evidence; (4) Mental health risks: SUBSTANTIAL evidence that cannabis use is likely to increase risk of developing schizophrenia and other psychoses (higher risk for more frequent users); MODERATE evidence of increased risk of suicide, suicidal ideation, and suicidal attempts; (5) Adolescent cognition: SUBSTANTIAL evidence linking adolescent cannabis use and negative outcome for learning, memory, and processing speed. The report’s finding on chronic pain is the primary citation for that specific claim in the cannabis section of this toolkit.
203.Marcus A. Bachhuber et al. (Brendan Saloner, Chinazo O. Cunningham, Colleen L. Barry), Medical Cannabis Laws and Opioid Analgesic Overdose Mortality in the United States, 1999–2010, 174 JAMA Internal Medicine 1668 (2014). PMID 25154332; PMC4392651; DOI 10.1001/jamainternmed.2014.4005. Time-series ecological analysis of medical cannabis laws and state-level opioid overdose mortality across all 50 states, 1999–2010 (including states where medical cannabis programs were effective prior to 1999: CA, OR, WA; plus 10 states that enacted laws 1999–2010). Key finding: states with medical cannabis laws had 24.8% lower mean annual opioid analgesic overdose mortality rate (95% CI −37.5% to −9.5%; p=0.003); the association generally strengthened over time after law implementation (years 1–6: −19.9% to −33.3%). Critical limitations of this study: ecological design (cannot establish individual-level causation); 1999–2010 data predates the high-potency opioid era (fentanyl); more recent analyses (e.g., Shover et al. 2019, PNAS) with longer follow-up have found that the initial protective association weakened or reversed as the opioid crisis shifted from prescription opioids to illicit fentanyl. Accurate statement: ‘Bachhuber et al. (2014) found a 24.8% lower mean annual opioid overdose mortality in states with medical cannabis laws 1999–2010, but more recent analyses with longer follow-up have weakened this finding.’ Do not state the substitution effect as established causal fact.
204.James W. Fernandez, Bwiti: An Ethnography of the Religious Imagination in Africa (Princeton University Press, Princeton, NJ, 1982). The definitive anthropological account of the Bwiti religious tradition of Gabon, Cameroon, and the Congo basin: covers the history, theology, ritual structure, and pharmacological role of iboga (Tabernanthe iboga) in Bwiti initiation ceremonies. Key finding relevant to this toolkit: high-dose iboga ingestion is central to Bwiti initiation, producing a 12–36 hour visionary experience described by practitioners as essential for spiritual transformation; the tradition represents one of the most thoroughly documented sacred plant ceremonies in sub-Saharan Africa. Ibogaine (the primary active alkaloid of iboga) in Bwiti traditional use is categorically distinct from Western medical use in setting, intention, preparation, and dose; the two should not be conflated in clinical or policy advocacy. See also: Goutarel R, Gollnhofer O, Sillans R (1993). ‘Pharmacodynamics and Therapeutic Applications of Iboga and Ibogaine.’ Psychedelic Monographs and Essays 6:70–111. For a medical review placing Bwiti tradition in context: Alper KR (2001). ‘Ibogaine: A review.’ Alkaloids Chem Biol 56:1–38. PMID 11705119. DOI 10.1016/S0099-9598(01)56005-8.
205.Jon Arcelus et al. (Alex J. Mitchell, Jackie Wales, Søren Nielsen), Mortality Rates in Patients with Anorexia Nervosa and Other Eating Disorders: A Meta-Analysis of 36 Studies, 68 Archives of General Psychiatry 724 (2011). PMID 21727255; DOI 10.1001/archgenpsychiatry.2011.74. Meta-analysis of 36 quantitative studies reporting mortality in eating disorders (AN: 166,642 person-years; BN: 32,798 person-years; EDNOS: 22,644 person-years). Key findings: weighted mortality rate 5.1 per 1,000 person-years for AN; standardized mortality ratio (SMR) 5.86 for AN (nearly 6 times the expected mortality for age- and sex-matched population controls) — the highest SMR among all psychiatric disorders. BN SMR: 1.93; EDNOS SMR: 1.92. One in five AN deaths by suicide. Age at assessment was a significant predictor of mortality. The finding that AN has the highest SMR of any psychiatric disorder — including schizophrenia, bipolar disorder, PTSD, and depression — is the primary empirical basis for the claim in Section XXVI that ‘anorexia nervosa has the highest mortality rate of any psychiatric condition.’ The ‘5–10% per decade’ characterization in the toolkit is consistent with this literature (5.1 deaths per 1,000 person-years = ~5% per decade, plus suicide adds additional mortality; long-term follow-up studies report cumulative lifetime mortality up to 10% in severe chronic AN).
206.Clive R. Bramham and Elhoucine Messaoudi, BDNF Function in Adult Synaptic Plasticity: The Synaptic Consolidation Hypothesis, 76 Progress in Neurobiology 99 (2005). PMID 16099088; DOI 10.1016/j.pneurobio.2005.06.003. Definitive review of BDNF-TrkB as an activity-dependent regulator of synaptic plasticity in the adult brain. Key mechanisms: (1) BDNF-TrkB localization to glutamate synapses makes this system a dynamic, activity-dependent regulator of excitatory transmission; (2) BDNF activates distinct mechanisms for induction, early maintenance, and late maintenance (synaptic consolidation) phases of long-term potentiation (LTP); (3) In the synaptic consolidation model, BDNF tags active synapses for mRNA capture (including the immediate early gene Arc), while Arc translation defines a critical window for consolidation of those specific active synapses. The activity-dependence is the key mechanistic basis for the claim that ‘BDNF-TrkB signaling selectively strengthens active synapses’: BDNF is released in an activity-dependent manner preferentially at recently-fired synapses, and its downstream signaling drives protein-synthesis-dependent strengthening specifically at those tagged synapses rather than globally. This provides the neurobiological mechanism for why the content of the psychedelic therapy session (which determines which cognitive/emotional circuits are active) shapes which synaptic connections are potentiated during the psilocybin-induced neuroplastic window.
207.Hyungju Park and Mu-ming Poo, Neurotrophin Regulation of Neural Circuit Development and Function, 14 Nature Reviews Neuroscience 7 (2013). PMID 23254191; DOI 10.1038/nrn3379. Authoritative review of BDNF as an activity-dependent regulator of neural circuits: ‘The expression, secretion and actions of BDNF are directly controlled by neural activity, and secreted BDNF is capable of mediating many activity-dependent processes in the mammalian brain, including neuronal differentiation and growth, synapse formation and plasticity, and higher cognitive functions.’ Establishes the Hebbian-style activity-dependence of BDNF/TrkB signaling — BDNF secretion is driven by calcium influx at recently active synapses, and TrkB activation in turn selectively strengthens those synapses via downstream kinase cascades (PI3K, MAPK/ERK). Complements Bramham & Messaoudi (Note 206) for the psychedelic-therapy context: the therapeutic session determines which cognitive circuits are active, and BDNF-TrkB signaling strengthens those specific active synaptic connections during the psychedelic-induced neuroplastic window.
208.Gaston Guzmán, J.W. Allen, and Jörg Gartz, A Worldwide Geographical Distribution of the Neurotropic Fungi, an Analysis and Discussion, 14 Annali del Museo Civico di Rovereto, Sezione: Archeologia, Storia, Scienze Naturali 189 (1998). Not PubMed-indexed (regional museum journal); no DOI. The definitive enumeration of psilocybin-containing and neurotropic fungi: catalogs 216 known species of neurotropic fungi worldwide, comprising Psilocybe (116 species), Gymnopilus (14 species), Panaeolus (13 species), Copelandia (12 species), Hypholoma (6 species), Pluteus (6 species), Inocybe (6 species), Conocybe (4 species), and Panaeolina (4 species), among others. The ‘approximately 200’ figure in the toolkit rounds the 216 neurotropic species count. Important taxonomic caveat: ‘216 neurotropic species’ includes species in multiple functional categories (psilocybin/indole-active species predominate, but the count also includes some ibotenic-acid and other neurotropic mushrooms); physico-chemically confirmed psilocybin species are fewer than 216 but comparable. Psilocybe remains the predominant genus. See also Van Court RC, Wiseman MS, Meyer KM et al. (2022), Frontiers in Forests and Global Change, for a modern peer-reviewed phylogenomic treatment. Building on Guzmán’s foundational 1983 monograph: The Genus Psilocybe. Beihefte zur Nova Hedwigia 74 (1983). Available via Fondazione Museo Civico di Rovereto archives.
209.Randall T. Brown et al. (Paul R. Hutson, corresponding author; University of Wisconsin-Madison), Pharmacokinetics of Escalating Doses of Oral Psilocybin in Healthy Adults, 56 Clinical Pharmacokinetics 1543 (2017). PMID 28353056; DOI 10.1007/s40262-017-0540-6. Open-label pharmacokinetics study (n=12 healthy adults; escalating oral doses 0.3, 0.45, and 0.6 mg/kg psilocybin; blood and urine over 24 h; LC-MS/MS). Key findings: (1) no psilocybin detected in plasma or urine — confirming complete hepatic first-pass dephosphorylation to psilocin before systemic circulation; (2) renal clearance of intact psilocin accounted for less than 2% of total clearance — confirming that psilocin undergoes primarily hepatic (not renal) elimination; (3) psilocin elimination half-life: 3 h (SD 1.1); (4) extended elimination phase in some subjects consistent with hydrolysis of psilocin glucuronide metabolite, confirming UGT-mediated glucuronidation as a primary elimination pathway; (5) no dose reduction needed for mild-moderate renal impairment. Provides the pharmacokinetic basis for the toolkit’s ‘Metabolism: hepatic via MAO and UGT; renal excretion’ statement. Paired with Hasler et al. 2004 (Note 210) for the onset/peak/duration timing data.
210.Felix Hasler, Ulrike Grimberg, Marco A. Benz, Theo Huber, and Franz X. Vollenweider (Heffter Research Center, Zurich), Acute Psychological and Physiological Effects of Psilocybin in Healthy Humans: A Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled Dose-Effect Study, 172 Psychopharmacology 145 (2004). PMID 14615876; DOI 10.1007/s00213-003-1640-6. Double-blind placebo-controlled dose-effect study (n=8 subjects; five oral psilocybin doses: placebo, 45, 115, 215, and 315 μg/kg body weight). Key time-course findings confirming the toolkit’s stated parameters: psychedelic effects onset in approximately 20–40 minutes; peak effects at approximately 60–90 minutes post-dose; total duration approximately 4–6 hours. Dose-dependent increases across all five ‘Altered States of Consciousness Rating Scale’ (5D-ASC) dimensions; attention impairment at medium and high doses; moderate blood pressure elevation at high dose only; no EKG or body temperature effects; TSH, ACTH, and cortisol elevated at peak high-dose effects. Study provides no cause for concern regarding somatic health. Paired with Brown et al. 2017 (Note 209) for the complete psilocybin ADME profile.
211.R. Gross-Isseroff, D. Salama, M. Israeli, and A. Biegon (Weizmann Institute of Science), Autoradiographic Analysis of Age-Dependent Changes in Serotonin 5-HT2 Receptors of the Human Brain Postmortem, 519 Brain Research 223 (1990). PMID 2397409; DOI 10.1016/0006-8993(90)90081-l. Primary human postmortem autoradiographic study of age-dependent changes in 5-HT2 receptor density using [3H]ketanserin (n=12 subjects for regional analysis; n=23 prefrontal cortical samples for Bmax/Kd). Key finding directly relevant to the toolkit’s claim: ‘In the prefrontal cortex and hippocampal dentate gyrus of 12 normal subjects, label density decreases sharply with age over the 2nd and 3rd decades,’ reaching a minimum around age 50. Saturation binding confirms the decrease reflects reduced receptor density (Bmax), not reduced affinity (Kd). Sex, alcohol, and postmortem delay had no significant effect. This establishes that 5-HT2A receptor density is highest in adolescence/early adulthood (2nd decade) and declines through the 3rd decade — supporting the claim that adolescent cortex has higher 5-HT2A density than adult cortex. Caveat: [3H]ketanserin is not perfectly 5-HT2A-subtype-selective; the ligand was the gold-standard available at the time of the study.
212.Evelyn K. Lambe, Stu G. Fillman, Maree J. Webster, and Cynthia Shannon Weickert, Serotonin Receptor Expression in Human Prefrontal Cortex: Balancing Excitation and Inhibition Across Postnatal Development, 6 PLoS One e22799 (2011). PMID 21829518; PMC3146513; DOI 10.1371/journal.pone.0022799. mRNA expression study of six serotonin receptor subtypes (HTR1A, HTR2A, HTR2C, HTR4, HTR5A, HTR6) across postnatal human prefrontal cortex (postmortem tissue across the lifespan from infancy to age 63). Key finding for HTR2A specifically: ‘low level of expression in infancy, increases during the infant years, plateaus in the toddler to teenager period, and then declines to adult levels.’ This mRNA-level developmental pattern corroborates the protein-level Bmax findings of Gross-Isseroff et al. 1990 (Note 211), establishing a peak of 5-HT2A expression in adolescence that declines to adult levels. The developmental changes suggest HTRs collectively maintain an inhibitory-excitatory balance across development; abnormal expression of individual HTRs may create windows of psychiatric vulnerability. This is the human prefrontal mRNA evidence base for the toolkit’s claim that 5-HT2A receptors are expressed at higher density in adolescent than adult cortical neurons.
213.M.S. Lidow, P.S. Goldman-Rakic, and P. Rakic (Yale University School of Medicine), Synchronized Overproduction of Neurotransmitter Receptors in Diverse Regions of the Primate Cerebral Cortex, 88 Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences 10218 (1991). PMID 1658799; PMC52899; DOI 10.1073/pnas.88.22.10218. Landmark primate (rhesus monkey) developmental receptor study: receptor density for dopaminergic, adrenergic, serotonergic, cholinergic, and GABAergic receptors reaches maximum between 2 and 4 months of age (equivalent to human late childhood/early adolescence in developmental terms) and then declines gradually to adult levels. Key finding: receptor overproduction is synchronized across diverse cortical regions, occurring in parallel with synaptogenesis. Establishes the evolutionary-conserved mechanism of adolescent receptor overproduction followed by pruning to adult levels — the primate developmental precedent for the human 5-HT2A adolescent-to-adult density reduction documented in Notes 211 and 212. Caveat: primate data not directly equivalent to human adolescence; the peak at 2-4 months in rhesus corresponds to a period of high neuroplasticity that maps approximately onto human adolescence/early adulthood.
214.B.J. Casey, Nim Tottenham, Conor Liston, and Sarah Durston (Sackler Institute for Developmental Psychobiology, Weill Medical College of Cornell University), Imaging the Developing Brain: What Have We Learned About Cognitive Development?, 9 Trends in Cognitive Sciences 104 (2005). PMID 15737818; DOI 10.1016/j.tics.2005.01.011. Neuroimaging review of cognitive brain development: establishes that association cortex (including prefrontal cortex) matures last in the human developmental sequence, with basic sensory-motor regions maturing first and association areas involved in top-down behavioral control maturing latest. Key finding underlying the toolkit’s ‘approximately age 25’ claim: prefrontal cortex maturation in functional connectivity and structural architecture is ongoing throughout adolescence into early adulthood (approximately the third decade). This is the foundational developmental neuroscience citation for the claim that ‘brain development is not complete until near the age of 25,’ and specifically that prefrontal-limbic connectivity governing emotional regulation is still developing during adolescence.
215.Mariam Arain et al. (Saint James School of Medicine), Maturation of the Adolescent Brain, 9 Neuropsychiatric Disease and Treatment 449 (2013). PMID 23579318; PMC3621648; DOI 10.2147/NDT.S39776. Review of adolescent brain maturation: establishes that myelinogenesis and prefrontal-limbic neurocircuitry remain structurally and functionally vulnerable through adolescence; ‘brain development is not complete until near the age of 25 years refers specifically to the development of the prefrontal cortex.’ Glutamatergic neurotransmission predominates in adolescence while GABAergic inhibitory tone is still under construction — contributing to impulsive behavior and immature emotional regulation. The immature limbic system and prefrontal cortex create the specific window of vulnerability documented in the cannabis adolescent risk section of this toolkit. Paired with Casey et al. 2005 (Note 214) for the developmental neuroscience basis of the ‘approximately age 25’ claim for prefrontal cortex maturation.
216.Anja Loizaga-Velder and Rolf Verres, Therapeutic Effects of Ritual Ayahuasca Use in the Treatment of Substance Dependence—Qualitative Results, 46 Journal of Psychoactive Drugs 63 (2014). PMID 24830187; DOI 10.1080/02791072.2013.873157. Qualitative empirical study (interviews with 13 therapists applying ayahuasca for addictions [4 indigenous healers + 9 Western mental health professionals], 2 expert researchers, and 14 individuals who underwent ayahuasca-assisted therapy; diverse contexts in South America). Key finding: ‘Ayahuasca can serve as a valuable therapeutic tool that, in carefully structured settings, can catalyze neurobiological and psychological processes that support recovery from substance dependencies and the prevention of relapse.’ The study provides empirically grounded hypotheses on therapeutic mechanisms: ayahuasca-facilitated introspection enables processing of traumatic material underlying addictive behavior; the ritual/ceremonial context amplifies therapeutic outcomes; treatment results are influenced by setting, therapist preparation, and integration support. Critical limitations: qualitative design (not quantitative); self-selected population; diverse and uncontrolled settings; therapist perspectives may reflect selection bias toward positive outcomes. This is the primary qualitative primary source on ayahuasca-assisted therapy mechanisms for addictions. Correction note: this is sometimes incorrectly cited as ‘Therapeutic insights from ayahuasca... 46(5):367–75’ — the correct pagination is 46(1):63–72 and the correct title is as cited here.
217.Mahmoud A. ElSohly and Waseem Gul, Constituents of Cannabis Sativa. In: Roger G. Pertwee, ed., Handbook of Cannabis 3–22 (Oxford University Press, Oxford, 2014). DOI 10.1093/acprof:oso/9780199662685.003.0001. The updated successor to ElSohly & Slade 2005 (Life Sci 78:539, PMID 16199061), incorporating an additional decade of cannabinoid identification: ‘a total of 104 cannabinoids (isolated or reported to date)... This brings the total number of constituents identified in cannabis to 545 compounds.’ The 104-cannabinoid count is the source for the toolkit’s ‘over 100 cannabinoids’ claim. Contrast with ElSohly & Slade 2005 (which documented only 70 cannabinoids and 489 total constituents) — do not cite the 2005 paper for the ‘over 100’ figure. The increasing count over time reflects improved analytical chemistry, not changes in the plant itself. See also Russo EB (2011) Br J Pharmacol 163(7):1344 (PMID 21749363) for the clinical pharmacology of the cannabinoid-terpenoid entourage effect; and Hanuš LO et al. (2016) Nat Prod Rep 33:1357 for cannabinoid structural classification.
218.Ruth A. Lanius, Robyn L. Bluhm, N.J. Coupland et al. (University of Western Ontario), Default Mode Network Connectivity as a Predictor of Post-Traumatic Stress Disorder Symptom Severity in Acutely Traumatized Subjects, 121 Acta Psychiatrica Scandinavica 33 (2010 [published online 2009]). PMID 19426163; DOI 10.1111/j.1600-0447.2009.01391.x. Resting-state fMRI study (n=11 acutely traumatized subjects): ‘resting state connectivity of the PCC with the perigenual anterior cingulate and the right amygdala is associated with current PTSD symptoms, and correlation with the right amygdala predicts future PTSD symptoms.’ Establishes that DMN (specifically posterior cingulate cortex/precuneus connectivity) is disrupted in PTSD in a manner that correlates with and predicts symptom severity. Wording note: PTSD is characterized by altered DMN connectivity — specifically, abnormal coupling between PCC and amygdala/perigenual ACC — rather than simple DMN hyperactivation; this paper documents increased PCC-amygdala coupling (not global DMN hyperactivation). For the toolkit’s purposes, ‘altered DMN connectivity’ is the more precise characterization for PTSD; the specific coupling between the DMN’s posterior node and the amygdala is the most replicated finding.
219.Robyn L. Bluhm, Peter C. Williamson, Elizabeth A. Osuch et al. (Ruth A. Lanius, corresponding author; University of Western Ontario), Alterations in Default Network Connectivity in Posttraumatic Stress Disorder Related to Early-Life Trauma, 34 Journal of Psychiatry & Neuroscience 187 (2009). PMID 19448848; PMC2674971. Resting-state fMRI study (n=17 female PTSD patients with early-life trauma + 15 healthy female controls): ‘Spontaneous activity in the default network during rest, as measured using PCC correlations, is altered in patients with PTSD.’ Specifically: PCC/precuneus connectivity with other default network regions was LOWER in PTSD patients than in healthy controls; PTSD patients also showed reduced PCC connectivity with right amygdala and hippocampus/parahippocampal gyrus — regions previously associated with PTSD. This counter-intuitive finding (reduced, not increased, default network connectivity in some PTSD presentations) underscores that PTSD is characterized by complex DMN reorganization, not simple hyperactivation. The observed alterations may be associated with disturbances in self-referential processing characteristic of PTSD. Caveat: Sample is limited to women with chronic early-life trauma; generalizeability to male patients or single-trauma exposure is limited. Paired with Lanius et al. 2009 (Note 218) as the two primary PTSD-specific DMN connectivity studies.
220.Rui Zhang and Nora D. Volkow (National Institute on Alcohol Abuse and Alcoholism / National Institute on Drug Abuse, NIH), Brain Default-Mode Network Dysfunction in Addiction, 200 NeuroImage 313 (2019). PMID 31229660; DOI 10.1016/j.neuroimage.2019.06.036. The definitive review of DMN dysfunction across substance use disorders (SUD): ‘Aberrant patterns of brain functional connectivity in the default mode network (DMN) have been observed across different classes of substance use disorder and are associated with craving and relapse.’ Key finding for addiction-specific DMN characterization: (a) anterior DMN RSFC tends to be DECREASED in addiction (impaired attribution of personal value and emotional regulation); (b) POSTERIOR DMN RSFC tends to be INCREASED (heightened internal attention — the mechanism underlying craving and rumination). This distinguishes the addiction-specific DMN pattern from the PTSD pattern (Note 218-219) and supports the toolkit’s ‘DMN hyperactivation’ framing for addiction (specifically the posterior DMN increase) while noting that anterior DMN shows the opposite. Additional findings: disrupted DMN-executive control and DMN-salience network dynamics; DMN RSFC as a potential biomarker for predicting clinical outcomes in SUD; DMN is identified as a target for addiction treatment. Validates the mechanistic logic for psychedelic therapy: psilocybin’s acute DMN disruption (Carhart-Harris 2012, Note 4) followed by post-treatment DMN reorganization (Carhart-Harris 2017, Note 99) may interrupt the aberrant DMN hyperactivation-in-posterior-regions pattern documented in addiction.
221.Amanda A. Nunes et al. (Jaime E.C. Hallak, corresponding; University of São Paulo), Effects of Ayahuasca and its Alkaloids on Drug Dependence: A Systematic Literature Review of Quantitative Studies in Animals and Humans, 48 Journal of Psychoactive Drugs 195 (2016). PMID 27230395; DOI 10.1080/02791072.2016.1188225. PubMed-based systematic review of quantitative studies on ayahuasca/alkaloid effects on drug-related symptoms or disorders. Found 5 animal studies and 5 human observational studies: all 5 animal studies showed improvement in biochemical or behavioral parameters related to drug disorders; of 5 human studies, 4 reported significant reductions in dependence symptoms or substance use, 1 showed no significant results. ‘The mechanisms responsible for the anti-addictive properties of ayahuasca and its alkaloids are not clarified, apparently involving both peripheral MAO-A inhibition by the β-carbolines and central agonism of DMT at 5-HT2A receptors expressed in brain regions related to the regulation of mood and emotions.’ Conclusion: ‘although results are promising, controlled studies are needed to replicate these preliminary findings.’ This is the most comprehensive systematic review of the ayahuasca-addiction evidence base through 2016, providing the quantitative counterpart to the qualitative evidence in Loizaga-Velder & Verres 2014 (Note 216) and Thomas et al. 2013 (Note 188).
222.Rafael Faria Sanches et al. (Jaime E.C. Hallak and José Alexandre S. Crippa, corresponding; University of São Paulo), Antidepressant Effects of a Single Dose of Ayahuasca in Patients With Recurrent Depression: A SPECT Study, 36 Journal of Clinical Psychopharmacology 77 (2016). PMID 26650973; DOI 10.1097/JCP.0000000000000436. Open-label inpatient trial (n=17 patients with recurrent depression; single oral dose of ayahuasca 2.2 mL/kg; HDRS, MADRS, BPRS, YMRS, CADSS evaluations at 80 min, day 1, 7, 14, and 21; SPECT brain perfusion at 8 h). Key findings: ‘significant score decreases in depression-related scales (HDRS, MADRS, BPRS) from 80 minutes to day 21’; increased blood perfusion in left nucleus accumbens, right insula, and left subgenual area 8 hours post-administration; well tolerated (vomiting in 47%, only AE noted). Ayahuasca may have ‘fast-acting and sustained antidepressive properties.’ This is the largest open-label trial and the primary SPECT neuroimaging study of ayahuasca for depression. Critical limitations: open-label design without placebo control; naturalistic ceremonial preparation (variable DMT/β-carboline ratio); subjective rating scales as primary endpoints; n=17. Complements the addiction-focused evidence of Notes 188, 216, and 221 by establishing that ayahuasca also has preliminary evidence for depression — the mood component underlying many substance use disorders.

Destigmatization Toolkit • Revised Edition v45 • Current as of June 2026 • Recommended re-verification cycle: every 90 days, pending FDA NDA reviews, the DEA cannabis hearing opening June 29, 2026, COMP005/COMP006 Part B durability data expected Q3 2026, and any new MDMA NDA submission by Resilient Pharmaceuticals.

About the Author

RN Collins (she/they) is a legal researcher, writer, and advocate working at the intersection of psychedelic law, cannabis policy, health equity, and cognitive liberty. She holds an M.S. and is pursuing a J.D., and currently serves as a legal intern at Antithesis Law PC in Venice, California — a boutique firm specializing in psychedelic and cannabis law.

RN's work spans primary source verification, legal research, policy analysis, science communication, and public education. She approaches this field from the conviction that accurate information is itself a form of access — and that the populations most harmed by psychoactive plant medicine stigma are precisely those most disconnected from the clinical, legal, and institutional infrastructure that shapes policy.

She is particularly focused on equity gaps in psychedelic research and access, Indigenous sovereignty and bioprospecting concerns, LGBTQ+ inclusion in clinical frameworks, and the intersection of cognitive liberty with federal drug scheduling.

About This Toolkit

The Destigmatization Toolkit for Psychoactive Plant Medicine was produced by RN Collins in collaboration with Antithesis Law PC. It represents nineteen audit cycles, 222 primary-source citations verified against original papers, and a multi-audience design built on the conviction that the same facts need to be communicated differently to land for a policymaker, a clinician, a concerned parent, and a harm reduction worker.
